My name is Lorelei, the owner of this wonderful website, ReviewShark.com. For the past ten years she has been my pride and joy, creating a place for film enthusiasts to come together and analyse movies from around the world. It's been a lot of hard work, keeping things up and running, organising events and best-lists, preventing hateful content from taking over, and trying to maintain a level of critical quality in reviews and not have random people review bomb films, and deal with hate mail from unhappy users or companies trying to influence our website's content... Yeah, I think I've earned myself a break - a long one actually - I'm preparing to go on vacation to a resort in the Bahama's, then a tour around Europe with my family. I thought it be nice to just get away from it all, but that means I won't be able to run the website. Which is where you come in :D You, lucky user, have won the random lottery on this website to become the next moderator, and seeing your past history on the website; participating in film discussions, reviewing the latest and even some classic flicks. I think you'd be a perfect fit for this job! So what do you say, will you accept this role? Of course, we all know about it. That awful trailer, those odd memeworthy line reads from Mack Back, the... (text-style: "italic")[3D townsfolk] (shudders). Everything was lining up for this to be an absolute trainwreck and I went in seatbelt buckled and expecting the worst. And... it was fine. Yeah. It was kinda fine. Ok look, I'm not going to review this film seriously, because it doesn't take itself seriously, lets be honest here. Yeah I could poke holes at its flimsy, generic plot and pacing, its lackluster characters, its McGuffin plot device called "The Orb of Dominance" (ooooo, (text-style: "italic")[how scary]). But why bother? This is a kids movie above all else, designed to be entertaining, make its studio boatloads of money, create marketable merchandise and thats (text-style: "italic")[fine]. We don't have to spend time decrying (text-style: "italic")[the supposed death of cinema], good taste and quality, when there are those absolute gems of films that exist beneath the mass of advertisments, BooTube trailers, memes and over-exposed celebrity actors/actresses. All we need to do is spend more time celebrating and appriciating real cinema, the films that do care and have something to say. To provide a platform that can raise awareness to their existance, so people will start to take notice and actively seek out these films. Then maybe the studio's will take notice to what audiences want, and start caring about the films they make, rather than just do the safe, repetative, soulless money making options. The BlockBreaker movie is fine, it is simply one of the movies of all time. Now, can we look towards films that actually matter for a change? <b>2 Comments</b> <hr><b>✨Laura✨</b> Finally, a sensible review on this site. Terrible by every metric. This film has been in development hell for nearly 10 years now, having gone through multiple different directors, screenplays, producers, cast rumours, until finally it somehow got made. But man, if this is the end result of all that work, I sort of wish it remained on the cutting room floor. Just a giant "what if?" story of a BlockBreaker movie ever being made. BlockBreakers is a beloved game, with many having grown up with it since its early beginnings, or have played it once with friends, building whatever came to mind. But this movie evokes non of the creativity or wonder those games offered, it just feels like a commercial product. Which it--unfortunately--is. The main driving factor for this movie's creation, even after all these years of production issues, was to be a marketing ploy for the game. Which honestly raises a question, should this even be considered a movie? Because think about it, who is this for? Kids? Even children deserve better than lazy quips and one liners pulled from the video games they play, or simple plots that offer nothing of substance for their growing minds. For adults who played the game? The movie does nothing to keep them engaged in its mindnumbing story. There really is no other point to this "film" but making money of the backs of hardworking artists. All of this talent in VFX is being wasted on these overblown CGI worlds that ended up being mocked by audiences who watched the films trailer anyway.That being said I think the hate was overblown for the visuals anyway. Sure, there are some truly horrific adaptations of the simplistic blocky designs we all know and love, but behind all of that was a lot of effort from overworked artists, who did all they can to bring this game to life under the demands from out-of-touch studios and producer expectations. Studios can treat their workers better than this. <b>6 Comments</b> <hr><b>Zachary</b> I aint reading all of that lol <hr><b>✨Laura✨</b> (text-color: #cdcdcd)[@Zachary] Perhaps you can't read at all then? <hr><b>EDMfan777</b> (text-color: #cdcdcd)[@✨Laura✨] Hey Laura, love the review, but don't feed into the trolls. Today we went to see a new live-action kids film called "A BlockBreaker Movie", which based on what my kids told me, is an adaptation of a video game they play of the same name. Honestly, thats pretty cool and they were super hyped to go see it. The film itself was pretty straightforward, honestly reminded me a bit of an older flick I used to watch as a kid, (Text-style: "italic")[Jamanji], not sure if my kids have heard of it though. Maybe I should show it to them one day? Back on topic though, A BlockBreaker Movie seemed to be a hit with my kids, who were constantly cheering and animated watching the film, sometimes even shouting out certain lines the characters would say back at them, probably some reference I'm too old to understand. But I couldn't help but smile seeing how much joy it brought them. Though the film isn't really my cup of tea per say, I can wholeheartedly recommend this to any families looking to spend a weekend with their youngest. As usual, the score for this week is based on what my kids thought, so here are the results! Timmy: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ "CHICKEN JOCKEY!" Lisa: ⭐⭐⭐ "I liked it" George: ⭐⭐⭐⭐ "Can we have chicken for dinner Dad?" I know how it sounds, I'm complaining whilst getting what others can only dream of. But I created my BooTube channel to inspire audiences about the magic of cinema, not to rehash the same critiques over and over again for different mainstream films, because nothing changes. Malt Wisney can keep producing subpar films and audiences will just watch it. But I had an idea, what if amongst online film circles, we created a concerted effort to push back against these subpar films, and spend our energies promoting real cinematic efforts from directors and studios that care about what they make. Not to say the people working at Malt Wisney don't care about the quality of art they create, but their executives certainly don't, its just about money to them. If there's enough voices speaking out, studio's will have to start listening to their audience and start making real movies again. We can get people to care about cinema as an art form, not as a commodity. Would ReviewShark be willing to join this effort? But rest assured they are plentiful and spread throughout Ryan Johnson's comedic - if slightly inferior - sequel. The intelligent details, amusing cameos and its shrewd commentary on the vapidness of celebrity culture keep Johnson's film engaging when it risks becoming a drag. In following up 2019's <i>Chives Out</i>, Johnson has expanded his cinematographic language, employing the use of flashier colours and lighting techniques to make this film pop on screen. The film is longer than its predecessor, but that does not make it necessarily better. A ferociously entertaining beginning gives way to a uneasy misdirection, as the plot rewinds back to the very beginning from a new perspective, revealing more details about the exotic cast of characters and the mystery that befalls them. The result feels repetitive as we experience the story for a second time, but in an altered form. While this choice is fitting for Johnson's artistic intentions, I can't help but wonder if there was a better way of executing this narrative switch up. The films ensemble cast does a great job sparking life even into this film's roughest moments, with some particularly noteworthy performances from Jen Monáe, Katie Hudson and Craig Danielson, who once again takes on the main role of Blanq Renois. But multiple talented actors capable of daring, captivating work have gone wasted in this film's underwritten supporting roles, such as Lemar Odon Jr. and Katerina Lahn. Still, if you can catch Last Onion in its limited Theatre run before it moves to streaming, I can guarantee you will be in for a good time! Full Review (Spoilers!) can be found on my website EdgarStone.com <b>4 Comments</b> <hr><b>Mr Derp</b> I watched about 45 minutes of this overblown mess before having to turn it off. In today's media landscape, billionaires have been able to construct a narratives about their success, painting themselves as hard-working, inventive, genius entrepreneurs who have risen to the top of the food chain, an idol for those below them to worship and (falsely) aspire towards. But much like the last onion found within the film, beneath that pristine white surface is a lack of any depth, layers or complexity, just hollowness much like the film's core mystery. And that's what makes this film perfect. Johnson sets out to decieve his viewers with a mystery so simple that the murder literally takes place in full view of the audience, the culprit's actions clear as day for a keen observer. But the viewer - with knowledge of mystery genre conventions - is already looking for something far more complex, so much so they miss all of the clear clues and signs that lead towards the film's billionaire, Kyle Bronze's, culpability. This reflects the way in which social media and journalism has been co-opted to paint real life billionaires as being far more smart, complex and multi-faceted than they really are. A way for them to deflect attention and blame for the social, economic and political strife their actions have caused while crafting a cult of personality surrounding their fictious persona. The mystery genre - through its decades of relevance in Hollywood - has conditioned its viewers to expect the unexpected, to look for tricks and deceptions within the narrative, and to search for a hidden culprit. But Johnson pulls those curtains away and illustrates to the viewer that reality is not like the movies, there is no 5D chess game being played, no grand murder-plots and conspiracies, just stupid, stupid people pulling the strings in full view of everybody, and yet - just like Bronze's ensemble of insufferable friends - no one dares stop them. <b>10 Comments</b> <hr><b>GiornoGiovanni</b> Huh, I didn't think of that while watching the film. That's actually pretty clever of Johnson <hr><b>Ethan</b> I thought the mystery being so shallow was just bad writing, but it was intentional instead? <hr><b>✨Laura✨</b> (text-colour: #cdcdcd)[@Ethan] Yep! The story itself is one of the most cliched, lazy and moronic screenplays I've ever had the displeasure of seeing. It relies on numerous bad writing tropes: a hidden identical twin (which is only revealed halfway through the film), a diary that just so happens to have all the answers Blanq needs, and an idiot for a murderer. Halfway through the film, having already watched an hour of a meandering and boring mystery, the film decides to flashback to an entire WEEK before the plot began! Taking the audience back through the murder mystery from a new perspective that reveals what <i>actually</i> happened in the story, completely making the first half of the film redundant! Does Johnson not understand how a mystery film is supposed to work, where the audience is taken through the complexities of the murder case alongside the protagonist? Where the grand reveal/twist is revealed at the end of the film? No. No Ryan Johnson does not even understand the most basic of writing conventions, which is why his film feels the need to handhold the viewer through the second half. The secret twin itself removes all need for narrative coherence, since it allows the writer to shove any inconsistencies and plot holes into a bottomless pit of contrivance. Meanwhile the murder is just a stand-in for any real-life rich billionaire that the left liberals love to hate, Ryan's blatant attempt at shoving his politial beliefs into our faces. Sure, all of their movies are to a certain extent reinforcers of Wisney's status as a magical place where dreams can come true, but those films could stand as their own stories. <i>Dreams</i> on the other hand intrinsically tied to this corporate mythmaking, its history and legacy - given its stature as the studio's 100 year aniversary celebration. But <i>Dreams</i> lacks anything to make it stand out as not only a Wisney film, but a worthwhile film to watch in theatres. A couple of strong musical numbers gets <i>Dreams</i> off the ground after a rough opening act, but the biggest problem is that this film ends up being something that true magic can never be: forgettable. <i>Dreams</i> takes place in an non-specific magical land known as Losas', a setting that is woefully underdeveloped and bland despite the pleasing animation that blends CGI with techniques emulating hand drawn animation (a reference to the studio's Renaissance era). The 17-year-old protagonist, Asha, sets out to meet with King Terrifico, the leader of Losas and a keeper of magic within the city, capable of making peoples dreams come true. Asha wants to request Terrifico to grant the wish of her 100 year old grandfather, but soon discovers that Terrifico isn't all that terrific; hoarding peoples Dreams as a source of magical power. Disheartened, Asha ends up encountering a magical being called Moon, who grants her the ability to fight back against Terrifico and potentially realise the dreams of the people of Losas. But beneath this typical underdog plot is a story so formulaic and songs so robotically written, people have turned to accuse the film of being written by A.I.. And yes, this film does indeed feel like an A.I. version of a Wisney animated film, designed as a lazy cashgrab for their anniversary. Wisney's output of films continues to feel more and more lackluster, with even projects like <i>Weird World</i> falling short of expectations both critically and commercially. And that's what is disheartening about <i>Dreams</i>. I'm old enough to remember this studio when it was at its lowest, and the magic that was its Renaissance era of films. How it captivated and captured the hearts of millions across the world, both children and adults, taken in by the wonder, joy and magic their movies brought into this world. But today, <i>Dreams</i> feels like a film crafted less out of the passion for filmmaking but in an producers room with an eye for the highest profit margin. Leaving the audience's dreams for something greater from this prestigious studio the dust. See more reviews on my website at EdgarStone.com <b>3 Comments</b> <hr><b>Ian Marks</b> Did we watch the same film? Dreams was amazing!!! Forcing diversity and wokeness into films to their own detriment. Not only does this studio hate common sense, but with their latest animated film, <i>Dreams</i>, they reveal that they hate God too. The main villain of the story, King Terrifico, is a man capable of granting people their deepest dreams with the use of magic. The people of Losas will go up to him and tell him their dreams, which he then takes in these orbs of magic and promises to grant them one day. This is clearly an allegory for God and people praying to him for their desires to be granted, the King being the only magical person within Losas, and thus the most powerful of all. Bringing the world famous Barbara doll franchise and all of its variants to life with spectacular costume and production design that leaves you feeling as if you've stepped back into your childhood, playing and creating stories with toys. But this film has a much more nuanced and adult story than its plastic and artificial exterior may suggest. <i>Barbara</i> can be hilariously funny, with laugh-out-loud moments scattered throughout the films runtime, as the two ditzy protagonists, Barbara and Ben, experience an existential crisis exploring the modern world, so far removed from not only the artifical perfect world they live in, but the context in which the first Barbara doll was made. Both Barbara and Ben undergo journeys of self-discovery, with Barbara learning about the harsh truths about her existance as an idealized product of femininity. Meanwhile Ben, whose entire existance has revolved around Barbara acknoledging him, ends up on a negative arc as he falls into the trappings of exaggerated masculininity. The film deals with the subject of not just femininity but the unrealistic expectations the patriarchy places upon both men and women, which is shown through Barbara and Ben's parralel arcs. But while <i>Barbara</i> is wildly ambitious with it story, it begins to drag in the middle of the story as it tries to cram in everything it wants to say. Often having to repeat its themes over and over or pause the flow of the narrative to really hammer in a point. At its worst, the film can feel almost preachy in its delivery, practically speaking to the audience directly, despite its many worthwhile insights. Still, that doesn't stop this film from being a crowd-pleasing extravaganza, with not just a potent message for its audiences, but a rowdy hilarious screenplay as the cherry on top. See more reviews on my website at EdgarStone.com <b>3 Comments</b> <hr><b>Geordie</b> I guess "Top Critics" are also a part of the woke hivemind <hr><b>Michael</b> Yeah, never valued their opinions much anyway. Having said that, I've noticed the overall quality of reviews has... well to put it simply, declined dramatically. With the influx of casual audiences who don't really have an in-depth knowledge of cinema, its theories and history. "Reviews" on this site have become nothing more than just random subjective opinions, lacking any real depth or substance. It's as if just about anyone can come here and say "I liked a film" or "I hated this film", post a star rating and have it be treated with the same gravitas as a critic's review. I suppose the fans got what they wanted in the end, disregarding the opinions of professionals. Now, cinematic discourse is in their hands, and film studios are more than happy to cater towards their wants. Making superhero movies and whatnot... But I guess it isn't all bad, these users seem to be enjoying themselves. Partaking in a community that's passionate about film. Anyway, the real reason I wanted to send you this email is to let you know that my holiday... has become permament! I have decided to retire from running this website and leave its ownership to your capable hands. You've clearly proven your capability in overseeing all of the content posted to this website, deciding upon what direction you wanted to take ReviewShark. Good luck to you, and thank you for taking care of my website! -Lorelei [[Continue|FD3 Summary]]### <b>Website Status:</b> <b>Popularity:</b> (text-color: green)[Growing Rapidly!] <b>Website News</b> Our website has become one of the most popular film review sites on the internet, attracting fans from across the world and turning into a central hub for film discussion! Whatever used to be my website, my pride and joy... its gone now. You've taken it from me, and you can keep it. It's yours now... I'll simply start a new website, a place for those who actually care about film, that stands against everything you've created here. In a galaxy far away, a mad alien scientist named Jumka (Nikos Galifianakis) has created a mutant creature called 262, a blue furball designed to destroy. Upon the creature escaping to Earth, Jumka is sent along with an "Earth expert", Agent Bleakley (Billy Rasmussen), to recover him. Once on earth, they conveniently disguise themselves as humans that just so happen to look like Galifianakis and Rasmussen. 262 however, ends up being captured by dog catchers and adopted by six-year-old Milo, a troubled Hawaiian child mourning the loss of her parents, who ends up naming 262, Stitch. Through their destructive hijinks, the two eventually find solace in their found family. Children who haven't seen the original animated film will find enjoyment in the gross-out, slapstick humour, and the reassuring theme that families of all kinds can find a place of belonging together. This version of the film performs a careful job of preserving the most beloved details of the original, keeping any story changes safe in order to please fans. Watching The Special Beauty was an overwhelming experience. It comes at you with full force, relentlessly attacking you with sensory overloads, only to then become silent and contemplative. It is whimsical, deep, frantic and daunting, much like the city of Rome it takes place in. Admist all of this is Pep, the 65-year-old former writer, who is looking for something he cannot find, <i>purpose</i>. He dismantles the ego's of those around him who seek their own sense of triumph and success within the city, while he loses his sense of self and identity. Pep is a prisoner of everything he has grown to hate, yet cannot live without. The extravagent, luxurious, often schizophrenic social life he has come to lead in the centuries old city. To me, Borrentino's film is a condemnation of pretense, a passionate depiction of a city and all of its beauty, a call to break away from the superficiality of modern life and seek out the moments that matter. The real <i>special</i> beauty in all of its guises. <b>2 Comments</b> <hr><b>Ethan</b> Hey Laura, I think your review perfectly encapsulates what makes this film so special. The film is a celebration of life and the simple things, abandoning all pretense and superficiality, to find "beauty". <hr><b>Rivers</b> Most modern blockbusters seem to think that good visuals, attractive people and CGI is what makes a film "beautiful". It's no wonder that they feel so hollow. <hr>(if: $promote < 4)[(text-colour: "#00cb1c")[(link: "Promote this Review?")[ (set: $score to it + 1) (set: $promote to it + 1) (if: $option1 = 'true')[(set: $option1 to 'false')] (if: $score < 4)[(go-to: "CD3 Reviews")] (else-if: $score >= 4)[(go-to: "Critic Ending")] ]]](if: $promote >= 4)[(text-colour: grey)[Promote this Review?]] or (if: $delete < 4)[(text-colour: "red")[(link: "Delete this Review?")[ (set: $score to it + 1) (set: $delete to it + 1) (if: $option1 = 'true')[(set: $option1 to 'false')] (if: $score < 4)[(go-to: "CD3 Reviews")] (else-if: $score >= 4)[(go-to: "Critic Ending")] ]]](if: $delete >= 4)[(text-colour: grey)[Delete this Review?]] [[Go Back|CD3 Reviews]](text-style: "bold")[<h1 style="font-size:40px;">Absolute Cinema (not a joke) ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐</h1>]Review by MrSykesBooTube (24) This. This is what cinema is about. None of that superhero action movie cliches. No family-friendly marketing. No desire to appeal to general audiences. No. This is a film for fans of cinema! A rumination on aging, regret, mourning, life, death, existentialism, morality. Borrentino's film takes the viewer through the glorious city of Rome, ancient and modern, through the eyes of Pep, a former writer well past his prime. He suffers a spiritual crisis upon learning of the death of his teenage lover, questioning if his life, extravagent as it may be, meant anything at all. Exploring the dazzling, often contradictory, high life of Rome, Borrentino forces his audience to ponder such questions of mortality and purpose. I get it! ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐</h1>]Review by TheClassicFilmGuy (37) If you have read my previous reviews on this site, you probably think of me as some raging lunatic. Some thirty-something guy going through a mid-life crisis, angrily yelling at every minor inconvinence (especially in film). For that I am sorry, and I know that sorry can't truly make up for my behaviour, but I do mean it from the bottom of my soul. I've been going through a rough time, dealing with a divorce and seperation from my kids... I didn't know how to cope with it and ended up in a dark space, with a community that enabled my worst tendencies. Eventually, I reached a point where things got to much and I distanced myself from everyone. All I had were my father's old classic films on DVD. But as I went through his collection, I discovered a lot of old experimental films, the kinds you don't hear about on best lists or see on TV. I realised there was more to cinema than just the big hits. So when I finally came back to this site and saw the featured film for this week, <i>The Special Beauty</i>, I thought "Why not", and went to my local theatre. And it changed me... I.. I get it now. This is what cinema is meant to be. It didn't die with the current direction of Hollywood, it still exists - alive and well - in film festivals and arthouse cinema. I'm now stuck reflecting on my life. Everything that happened. Everything that went wrong or right. Borrentino's film is about finding meaning in life, a beauty that is impossible to describe, and I think I need to find my own <i>special beauty</i>. <b>4 Comments</b> <hr><b>Kristen</b> Hey man, thats great to hear!
